SECTION 1
Protein Foods for Cricketers
500+ protein food options with 7 data fields each, organized by category.
Protein is the building block of every muscle fibre, tendon and ligament in a cricketer's body. It repairs micro-tears from fast
bowling, rebuilds strength after gym sessions and maintains the lean mass needed for explosive batting. Indian cricketers
have access to one of the richest protein-food environments in the world — from high-quality dairy and legumes to diverse
fish and poultry. The table below is organized by food category so athletes can easily find options that match their diet type,
budget and lifestyle.
Budget Key: Low = under n30/100g raw | Low-Med = n30–60 | Medium = n60–150 | High = above n150/100g Hostel:
Yes = available in mess or no-cook | Mostly = light prep needed | No = specialty source

SECTION 2
Carbohydrate Foods for Cricketers
500+ carbohydrate foods mapped to three cricket contexts: Match Day, Training & Recovery.
Carbohydrates are the primary fuel for cricket — a sport combining explosive sprints, sustained concentration, multi-hour
innings and bowling spells. Muscle glycogen (stored carbohydrate) is what powers every boundary sprint, diving catch and
final-over delivery. Indian athletes have access to an extraordinarily rich carbohydrate food environment. This section maps
500+ carbohydrate sources to three distinct cricket contexts: Match Day (controlled, timed fuelling), Training Day
(volume-based loading), and Recovery (glycogen restoration).

SECTION 3
Healthy Fats for Cricket Performance
220+ foods covering hormonal support, recovery acceleration & joint health.
Dietary fat is not the enemy of performance — it is a critical regulator of hormones (testosterone, cortisol, oestrogen), the
medium for fat-soluble vitamins (A, D, E, K), and a key structural component of every joint membrane in a cricketer's body.
Omega-3 fatty acids from fish and seeds reduce muscle inflammation after long bowling spells; saturated fats from dairy
support testosterone production; and monounsaturated fats from nuts lubricate joint capsules. The table below categorises
250+ healthy fat sources by their hormonal, recovery and joint-health actions.

SECTION 4
Focus & Concentration Foods
20 foods that directly improve decision-making, shot selection and sustained attention.
Cricket demands unbroken concentration for hours — a batsman facing 200 balls, a spinner working out a plan, a captain
reading field placements. Certain foods directly influence acetylcholine (the memory neurotransmitter), dopamine
(motivation + decision-making) and cerebral blood flow. Eat these regularly and eat them on match mornings.
Food Why It Helps Cricket Focus
Walnuts (30g daily) Richest omega-3 ALA nut; raises DHA in brain cortex fi faster signal transmission between neurons fi
better shot selection under pressure. Phenolic compounds reduce oxidative stress in prefrontal cortex
(decision centre). Studies show 13 weeks of walnut intake improves cognitive flexibility.
Blueberries (100g) Anthocyanins enter blood-brain barrier; enhance neuroplasticity and working memory. Pterostilbene
activates BDNF (brain-derived neurotrophic factor) — the brain's growth hormone. Bowlers and batsmen:
reduces brain fog in sessions beyond 3 hours.
Eggs – Whole (2 daily) Choline in yolk is direct precursor to acetylcholine, the neurotransmitter for memory, focus and muscle
control. Deficiency impairs concentration. DHA in yolk builds myelin sheath = faster nerve conduction =
quicker reaction to ball swing.
Dark Chocolate 70%+ (20g before match) Theobromine + flavanols fi vasodilation in cerebral arteries fi more oxygen/glucose to working brain.
Phenylethylamine boosts dopamine naturally. Takes effect in 30-45 minutes. Improves sustained attention
in cognitive tasks. Perfect 45 min pre-match.
Turmeric + Black Pepper (haldi doodh) Curcumin with piperine (95x bioavailability) crosses blood-brain barrier fi reduces neuroinflammation fi
reduces brain fog. Increases BDNF and serotonin. India's most practical brain food. 1 glass nightly.
Green Tea (1 cup, 2–3 hrs pre-match) L-theanine + caffeine synergy: caffeine for alertness + L-theanine for calm focus (prevents jitteriness).
Unlike coffee, no anxiety spike. Alpha brain-wave activity increases = relaxed concentration. Perfect
pre-match drink for batsmen who need long calm focus.
Banana (1 ripe, 30–60 min pre-match) Tyrosine fi dopamine chain: dopamine regulates motivation, confidence and decision speed. Glucose + B6
(highest fruit) activate neurotransmitter synthesis. Potassium prevents brain dehydration (1% dehydration =
5% drop in concentration).
Brahmi / Bacopa (5g powder or 30 leaves) Clinical herb: bacosides in Brahmi inhibit cholinesterase fi more acetylcholine available fi long-term
concentration. Studies show 12 weeks of Bacopa improves memory consolidation and speed of information
processing. Used by Indian scholars for centuries.
Amla / Indian Gooseberry (2 fresh or 10g
powder)
Richest natural Vit C food: 600–900mg/100g. Vit C reduces cortisol under pressure (cortisol destroys
focus). Vitamin C is required for dopamine synthesis. Antioxidants protect myelin sheath from oxidative
stress during long match days.
Ashwagandha (1/2 tsp in milk) KSM-66 root extract: reduces cortisol by 30% in clinical trials. High cortisol destroys working memory and
decision-making. Reduces match-day anxiety fi sharper focus. Withanolides also improve auditory-reaction
time. Classic Indian performance adaptogen.
Pumpkin Seeds (30g) Zinc deficiency = concentration problems. Pumpkin seeds highest zinc food (2.5mg/30g). Zinc cofactor for
300+ enzymes including those making dopamine and GABA. Magnesium reduces nervousness.
Tryptophan fi serotonin for calm match-day mood.
Spinach / Palak (100g) Nitrates fi nitric oxide fi cerebral vasodilation = more O2 to brain. Lutein deposits in visual cortex =
sharper, faster visual processing of ball trajectory. Iron prevents anaemia-related brain fog. Top focus
vegetable for Indian cricketers.
Oatmeal (100g at breakfast) Beta-glucan creates slow, steady blood glucose over 4 hours fi no energy crash mid-innings. Glucose is
the brain's only fuel — stable glucose = stable concentration. Thiamine (B1) in oats required for
acetylcholine synthesis. Best concentration breakfast.
Fatty Fish – Surmai/Tuna (100g) DHA is 40% of brain's grey matter. Low DHA = poor memory and slow decision-making. EPA reduces
neuroinflammation. Regular fish consumption (3x/week) increases grey matter density in areas controlling
attention. Most powerful long-term focus food.
Pomegranate Juice (200ml) Punicalagins cross blood-brain barrier; study: daily pomegranate juice for 28 days fi significant
improvement in learning and memory in middle-aged patients. Increases oxygen delivery to brain via nitric
oxide. Reduces cognitive decline from inflammation.
Food
Almonds – Soaked (10 daily)
Coffee / Chai (1 cup 2h pre-match)
Why It Helps Cricket Focus
Phenylalanine fi tyrosine fi dopamine/norepinephrine chain. Pre-soaked almonds: better bioavailability of
Vit E (protects neurons from oxidation) and riboflavin (energy metabolism in neurons). L-carnitine in
almonds supports mitochondrial function in brain cells.
Caffeine blocks adenosine receptors fi prevents drowsiness fi heightens alertness. Effect peaks at 60-90
min. Combined with L-theanine (masala chai) = calm focus. WARNING: Avoid within 6h of bedtime and
maximum 1 cup. Over-reliance = anxiety + crash.
Moringa Leaves (100g)
Lion's Mane Mushroom (10g dried)
Coconut Oil / MCT (1 tsp in morning)
Isothiocyanates and quercetin reduce brain inflammation. Contains 25 bioactive brain compounds including
GABA-precursors. Iron from moringa prevents anaemia-related brain fog. Studies show moringa extract
improves memory and reduces anxiety. India's brain superfood.
Only food proven to stimulate NGF (nerve growth factor) — grows new neural pathways. Available in India
as supplement. Improves focus, memory and reduces brain fog. Best taken as 8-week course before major
tournament. Premier brain mushroom.
MCTs fi ketones within 1-3 hours fi ketones cross blood-brain barrier and fuel neurons directly without
insulin. Brain fog drops rapidly. For batsmen facing long innings, MCT pre-match provides brain fuel that
lasts 4-6 hours without glucose fluctuation.

SECTION 5
Strength & Power Foods
20 foods that build batting power, bowling thrust and muscle development.
Batting power, bowling explosive thrust and throwing velocity all depend on maximal muscle force. Specific foods support
muscle protein synthesis, creatine phosphate regeneration, testosterone levels and muscle hypertrophy. These are not
magic foods — they work when combined with progressive resistance training and adequate sleep.
Food / Nutrient How It Builds Cricket Strength & Power
Chicken Breast (150g post-training) 31g complete protein/100g with all essential amino acids. Leucine content 2.7g/100g — the amino acid that
triggers mTOR pathway and turns on muscle protein synthesis. Eat within 30–45 minutes post-strength
training. Combined with fast carb, increases GH and IGF-1 by 30%.
Whey Protein Isolate (1 scoop post-training) Fastest absorbing complete protein — in bloodstream within 30 min. Highest leucine density of all proteins
(11%). Most researched supplement for muscle hypertrophy. 25g post-training + 25g carb is the single most
evidence-supported muscle-building strategy. Essential for fast bowlers rebuilding each day.
Eggs – 3 whole + 3 whites (morning) Leucine + choline + creatine + cholesterol for testosterone. Whole eggs outperform egg whites alone in
muscle building (yolk's cholesterol supports testosterone synthesis). Egg yolk contains creatine. Ideal
morning meal for any cricketer in strength phase.
Red Meat – Lean Mutton/Goat (100g 2x
weekly)
Highest creatine food (1g/100g raw meat). Creatine fi phosphocreatine fi ATP regeneration in fast-twitch
fibres (bowling, batting drives). Haem iron + zinc + Vit B12 = complete anabolic mineral complex. Carnitine
in red meat = 4x more fat-to-energy conversion for heavy training days.
Creatine Monohydrate (5g daily in water) Most researched legal supplement. Increases muscle phosphocreatine stores by 20%. Increases maximum
strength by 5–15% in trained athletes. More explosive throw velocity, more bowling pace. For cricket: take
daily, no loading required. Effects seen in 4 weeks. Safe for all Indian athletes.
Paneer (100g daily for vegetarians) 18g casein protein/100g — slow 7-hour digestion protects muscle from breakdown. Calcium activates
troponin in muscle contraction. CLA supports anabolic hormone function. Best vegetarian muscle food in
India. Ideal pre-sleep muscle food for veg athletes.
Soy Chunks (50g dry = 150g cooked) 52g protein/100g dry — highest protein plant food by far. Complete EAA profile. Isoflavones genistein and
daidzein support muscle recovery via oestrogen-receptor muscle pathway. 25g plant protein/meal triggers
same MPS rate as whey in trained athletes if leucine is adequate.
Quinoa (150g cooked) Only grain with all 9 EAAs and significant leucine content. When replacing white rice with quinoa
post-training, leucine-dependent muscle synthesis increases. Also contains ecdysteroids (plant compounds
that activate same pathway as anabolic steroids in muscle, without the side effects).
Beetroot Juice (200ml 2h before training) Nitrates fi NO fi vasodilation fi 1–3% increase in muscle power output in trained athletes. Reduces ATP
cost of each muscle contraction by 19% = more explosive force for less energy. Increases muscle creatine
uptake. Drink 90 min before strength training sessions.
Rajma / Kidney Beans (100g cooked) Arginine fi nitric oxide = more blood + oxygen to muscles. Plant protein 9g/100g + complex carb fi insulin
spike post-training = drives amino acids into muscle cells. Iron + zinc support testosterone pathway. Best
plant protein + carb combo for strength in North India diet.
Tuna Canned (100g) 26g protein/100g + selenium. Selenium activates GPx enzymes that prevent free-radical muscle damage
post-heavy lifting. B12 supports nerve signals to fast-twitch fibres. Zero prep time — ideal
post-strength-training protein snack without cooking.
Milk – Full fat (500ml) CLA + Vit D + casein + whey blend. Study: post-exercise milk outperforms sports drinks and soy for muscle
mass gain in young athletes. Insulin response from lactose drives amino acids to muscle. Affordable,
accessible, India-perfect strength drink.
Groundnuts / Peanuts (30g daily) Arginine fi NO fi greater blood flow to working muscles. Niacin/B3 activates NAD which is required for
ATP production in fast-twitch fibres. 26g protein/100g. Most affordable complete-amino snack. Resveratrol
in skin reduces DOMS after heavy training.
Banana + Whey Post-Training Banana glucose + fructose = rapid glycogen reload fi insulin spike fi insulin shuttles amino acids from
whey into muscle. Study: banana+protein post-training = equivalent to commercial recovery drinks.
Indian-affordable combination. Best post-batting or post-strength snack.
Pumpkin Seeds (30g) Zinc 2.5mg/30g = most bioavailable food zinc. Zinc is essential for testosterone production (Leydig cell
function). Magnesium activates over 300 enzymes including ATP synthase. Research: zinc + Mg
supplementation increases free testosterone and IGF-1 in trained athletes.
Food / Nutrient
Ashwagandha Root (600mg extract daily)
Sweet Potato (150g post-training)
How It Builds Cricket Strength & Power
KSM-66 study: 12 weeks fi 1.5–2x greater muscle mass gain vs placebo in resistance training. Reduces
cortisol (catabolic hormone) by 30%. Increases testosterone by 15–17%. Reduces exercise-induced muscle
damage (CK). Best legal botanical supplement for cricket strength.
High GI post-training fi insulin spike. Leucine + insulin combo = maximum mTOR activation. Beta-carotene
fi Vit A fi supports IGF-1 signal. Potassium prevents muscle cramping in heavy leg-training sessions for
fast bowlers. Best post-training vegetarian carb for strength.
Ragi / Nachni Flour (100g)
Horse Gram / Kulthi Dal (100g cooked)
Pomegranate Juice (200ml post-training)
Highest plant calcium. Calcium is required for every muscle contraction and for myosin-actin cross-bridge
activation. Adequate calcium = stronger more complete muscle contraction. Calcium deficiency = weaker
maximal force output regardless of training. For fast bowlers: calcium protects against stress fractures.
Highest protein Indian pulse at 10g/100g cooked. Phytic acid reduction by soaking = better iron absorption.
Iron carries oxygen to muscle (haemoglobin/myoglobin). More iron = more O2 to working muscle = more
ATP production = more force. Traditional South India strength food.
Punicalagin (ellagitannin) reduces DOMS by 13–16% in published studies. Reduces CRP and IL-6 markers
post-strength training. Increases blood flow to muscle (nitrates). Faster recovery = ability to train harder next
day = greater strength gains over time.

SECTION 6
Speed & Agility Foods
13 foods that improve sprinting, running between wickets and fielding speed.
Cricket speed is multi-faceted: the 2-second sprint between wickets, the fielder diving full-stretch, the outfield boundary
save. Speed is 80% neuromuscular — the ability to recruit fast-twitch muscle fibres, generate maximal force quickly, and
respond to a visual cue in under 200ms. These foods enhance fast-twitch recruitment, explosive power and the neural
speed that separates a run-out from a boundary.
Food How It Improves Cricket Speed & Agility
Beetroot / Nitrate Foods (200ml juice 90min
pre-match)
Nitrates fi NO fi reduces O2 cost of each sprint by 3–5% fi more sprints per match without fatigue.
Specifically: NO dilates type-II fast-twitch muscle blood vessels (not slow-twitch) fi more ATP delivered per
sprint. 6-day beetroot protocol increases sprint-repeat capacity by 4%.
Caffeine (1-3mg/kg, 60min pre-match) Blockades adenosine receptors fi releases motor neurons from inhibition fi faster neural firing rate fi
more motor units recruited fi more force per step. Shown to improve sprint speed by 1-3% and jump height
by 4-5% in controlled studies. Take 60min before match, not daily.
Creatine (5g daily) Most effective supplement for speed: phosphocreatine (PCr) regeneration speed is what limits repeated
sprint ability. More PCr = faster ATP between sprints. Shown to improve sprint time by 1–5% and total sprint
work by 12% in cricket-simulation protocols. Take daily.
Bananas + Dates (pre-match combination) Combined fructose + glucose = dual-transporter carbs fi 20% faster glycogen delivery to fast-twitch fibres
vs glucose alone. Dates provide quick-acting natural sugars. Bananas provide B6 for neurotransmitter
synthesis. Eat 30-45 min before match.
Tuna / Lean Protein (150g 2h pre-match) Tyrosine in tuna fi dopamine/norepinephrine fi faster neural response times (reaction speed). Protein
maintains fast-twitch muscle glycogen. Lean protein pre-match prevents muscle catabolism during intense
fielding sessions. Don't eat heavy protein immediately before sprint drills.
Watermelon (200g or 500ml juice
pre-training)
Citrulline fi arginine fi NO fi vasodilation in fast-twitch muscle vessels. Specific to citrulline: reduces
perceived muscle soreness 24h after speed sessions by 40%. Direct sprint recovery food. Also 92% water
— prevents speed loss from dehydration (2% dehydration = 10% speed drop).
Iron-Rich Foods (spinach + lobia + liver
3x/week)
Haemoglobin carries O2 to fast-twitch fibres. Low iron = anaemia = reduced VO2max = slower sprint top
speed and worse repeat sprint. India cricketers especially: iron deficiency is most common nutrient
deficiency. Weekly liver or daily spinach + Vit C = full iron.
Carbohydrate Loading Pre-Match
(rice+roti+banana)
Fast-twitch fibres run almost entirely on glycogen. When glycogen is low, fast-twitch fibres can't generate
maximal speed. 3x normal carb intake 24h before match = supercompensation. More stored glycogen =
more sprints maintained at top speed until the final overs.
Green Tea (1 cup with L-theanine) EGCG antioxidant + L-theanine: L-theanine increases alpha brain waves fi relaxed alertness fi faster
reactive speed (visual stimulus to motor response). Catechins increase fat oxidation, sparing glycogen for
sprint moments. Caffeine + L-theanine combo optimal.
Chia Seeds (30g soaked in water 1h
pre-training)
Hydrogel matrix: chia absorbs 9x weight in water fi slow-releases hydration during sprint sessions. ALA
omega-3 improves cell membrane fluidity fi faster electrical signal transmission in motor neurons fi
quicker reaction and muscle recruitment.
Moringa (100g leaves or 5g powder) Iron (28mg/100g dried = highest plant iron) + Vit C for absorption = haemoglobin + myoglobin fi oxygen
delivery to fast-twitch sprint muscle. Leucine content in moringa supports muscle protein maintenance
during multi-day tournaments. India's practical speed superfood.
Dark Leafy Greens – Spinach/Methi/Sarson
(daily)
Nitrates in leafy greens fi NO pathway. Magnesium in greens activates ATP synthase fi faster ATP
production for sprint bursts. Folate supports red blood cell production for oxygen delivery. Chlorophyll fi
iron fi haemoglobin. Daily greens = cumulative speed support.
Coconut Water (250ml during match) Natural isotonic: potassium 600mg + sodium + magnesium. Potassium is required for every nerve impulse
and muscle contraction. Low potassium = slower nerve conduction velocity = reduced sprint speed.
Electrolyte balance during match maintains top-end speed in 40°C Indian heat.

SECTION 7

15 foods and strategies specifically for fast bowlers and spin bowlers.
Bowling is the most physically demanding aspect of cricket. A fast bowler can cover 25km in a Test match day, deliver each
ball at near-maximum effort, and repeat this for 15–25 overs. Spin bowlers bowl more overs but face unique demands on
shoulder rotator cuffs, wrist flexors and standing endurance. This section covers nutrition specifically for bowling
performance and longevity.

SECTION 8
Batting Endurance Foods
15 foods for long innings, sustained concentration and stable session-long energy.
Long innings — from a patient half-century to a match-saving double century — place unique demands on a batsman. After
90 minutes at the crease, blood glucose drops and concentration narrows. After 3 hours, glycogen depletion alters reaction
time. After 5 hours, neural fatigue mimics the effect of slight intoxication on decision-making. The foods below sustain
mental clarity, stable blood glucose, visual sharpness and technical precision across a full day.
Food / Strategy How It Sustains Long Innings
Oatmeal Breakfast (Morning of match) Slow GI (55) releases glucose over 4 hours. Beta-glucan delays gastric emptying. Ensures stable blood
sugar for the first session without spikes or crashes. No dip in concentration in the first hour at crease — the
critical 'getting in' phase.
Banana Bunch Pocket (during innings) Eat 1 banana every 45–60 min at drinks breaks. Glucose + fructose = dual-transporter refuelling 20% faster
than glucose alone. Potassium replenishes sweat losses preventing muscle cramps in the legs (crucial for
footwork). B6 maintains dopamine focus.
Dates (3–4 during innings breaks) 90g per 100g natural sugar; highest natural sugar density. Iron + potassium + magnesium bundle. Small,
portable, no mess. Eat 2-3 dates per break = constant glycogen topping up without digestive disruption.
Proven pre-battle food in many cultures.
Honey in Warm Water (mid-innings drink) Glucose + fructose 50:50 = dual transporter carbs. Antimicrobial throat coating prevents vocal strain
(captains). Instant glycaemic response within 10 min. 1 tablespoon (17g carbs) in 300ml warm water = ideal
between-overs sip.
Jaggery (10–15g per interval) Iron-fortified natural sugar: replaces glycogen and iron simultaneously. Low GI vs white sugar. Traditional
India between-innings cricket snack. Molasses content provides B vitamins for nerve function. 1 small block
per session.
Coconut Water (200–300ml per break) Potassium 600mg per can = highest electrolyte drink. Restores the 2% fluid loss level before concentration
drops. Contains cytokinins which studies associate with cellular health during prolonged stress. Natural
isotonic suited to Indian climate.
Sports Gel (1 sachet during long innings) 22g pure glucose-maltodextrin; absorbed within 5-10 minutes; fast-acting glycogen topping. For 150+ ball
innings: take 1 gel every 60 minutes. Prevents the 'wall' (glycogen crash) that causes technical mistakes in
batting after 3 hours.
Whole Grain Roti + Dal (pre-match 3h) Slow GI complex carb from wheat + protein from dal = protein-modulated glycaemic release. The protein
blunts glucose spike fi provides 4h sustained energy rather than 2h from white rice alone. North India's
ideal pre-match combination.
Chia Seeds in Water (morning of match) Forms hydrogel: holds water + slow-releases carbs over 4-6 hours. ALA omega-3 improves visual cortex
cell membrane fluidity = clearer, faster visual processing of ball trajectory. Prevents dehydration-induced
concentration lapse in afternoon sessions.
Beet Juice (200ml 2h pre-batting) Nitrate-derived NO fi 15-20% increase in cerebral blood flow fi more oxygen to prefrontal cortex (decision
centre). Improves time-constrained cognitive decision-making by 2-5%. Physically: reduces O2 cost of
running between wickets.
Almonds (10, soaked, morning of match) Phenylalanine fi dopamine/norepinephrine. Riboflavin activates neurons in the visual cortex. Vit E protects
retinal cells and myelinated optic nerves. L-carnitine improves brain mitochondria energy = sustained focus
for final session of day. Ancient Indian cricketer's pre-match food.
Moringa Powder (5g in water pre-match) Most complete plant food: 9 EAA + iron + calcium + Vit C + quercetin. Quercetin reduces neuroinflammation
= less brain fog in long innings. Iron prevents anaemia fatigue. Vit C required for dopamine synthesis.
India's practical complete superfood.
Pumpkin Seed Snack (30g) Zinc + magnesium + tryptophan: tryptophan fi serotonin fi calm focus for the anxious batsman. Zinc
cofactor for 300+ brain enzymes. Magnesium blocks NMDA receptors = reduces anxiety without sedation.
Ideal pre-innings calming protein snack.
Dark Chocolate (20g 45 min pre-innings) Theobromine fi vasodilation; flavanols fi increase cerebral blood flow 8%; phenylethylamine fi dopamine
uplift. 45-minute effect window matches warmup fi innings sequence. The 'mindful' snack for high-pressure
batting situations.
Food / Strategy
Hydration Protocol (During Long Innings)
How It Sustains Long Innings
Dehydration of 1% body weight = 5% drop in concentration and 8% drop in technical skill. Target:
400-500ml/hour in Indian heat. Electrolyte drink (not plain water alone) to prevent hyponatremia. Urine
colour check: pale yellow = hydrated; dark = replace immediately.

SECTION 9
Wicket-Keeper Nutrition
15 foods targeting the unique demands of reaction time, agility and recovery.
Wicket-keeping combines the highest sustained concentration of any fielding position, the fastest reaction times on the
pitch, and the greatest physical demand of any specialist fielder: squatting, lunging, diving and exploding upright for 90+
overs. The wicket-keeper's nutritional needs blend the demands of the batsman (concentration + glycogen) with the
specialist requirement for sub-200ms reaction time and sustained explosive agility.
Food / Nutrient Reaction Time Agility Recovery
Omega-3 (fatty fish 3x/week) DHA speeds neural conduction in
visual pathways; reduces reaction
time by 20ms in studies
Reduces muscle soreness

SECTION 10
Recovery Foods
30 recovery foods mapped to three distinct cricket recovery scenarios.
Recovery is where performance gains are actually made. Training creates the stimulus; recovery creates the adaptation.
Cricket presents three distinct recovery scenarios, each with different nutritional demands: post-match (glycogen severely
depleted, psychological fatigue, possible physical injury), post-nets (moderate depletion, skill-focused session, muscle fibre
repair), and post-strength training (maximal muscle protein synthesis window, glycogen reload). The 30-minute window
after training is the most critical nutritional period of the day.

SECTION 11
Hydration Guide
Complete fluid strategy: water, electrolytes, coconut water, ORS, sports drinks.
Dehydration is the most commonly underestimated performance derailer in Indian cricket. Just 1.5% body weight in fluid
loss — less than one bottle of water — reduces batting concentration by 12%, fielding reaction time by 8%, and bowling
accuracy by 6%. In 40°C Indian summer conditions, a cricketer can lose 1–2 litres of sweat per hour. Yet most young
Indian cricketers drink far less than needed. This section provides a complete hydration framework for every cricket
scenario.

Critical Hydration Rules for Indian Cricketers
Instant glucose. Only post-match
(not during – too fast a spike). Iron
bonus
25g whey + banana + 400ml milk +
pinch salt = complete post-match
recovery drink
Use carefully: 1-3mg caffeine/kg
body weight 60 min pre-match.
Compensate with extra 200ml water
NEVER: delays glycogen synthesis
50%, impairs MPS 30%, disrupts
sleep recovery architecture
1. URINE COLOUR RULE: Pale yellow (like lemonade) = hydrated. Dark yellow = dehydrated. Colourless = overhydrated (rare
but real).
2. BODY WEIGHT METHOD: Weigh yourself before and after training. Every 1kg lost = 1 litre of sweat. Drink 1.5x that volume
over the next 3-4 hours.
3. SALT WITH WATER: Plain water alone during long matches actually dilutes blood sodium and can cause hyponatremia
(dizziness, nausea). Always add a pinch of salt or use electrolyte drinks during matches.
4. START HYDRATED: Never start a training session or match in a dehydrated state. Drink 500ml of water 2 hours before play
+ 200ml 30 min before.
5. HEADACHES = DEHYDRATION: Headaches during or after play are almost always dehydration. Treat with 500ml electrolyte
water before reaching for a painkiller.
6. CRAMPS = SODIUM: Most cramps during cricket are from sodium loss (not magnesium or potassium as commonly
believed). Add salt to your drinks.
7. HEAT INDEX AWARENESS: At 35°C + 80% humidity, effective temperature is 42°C. Increase fluid intake by 30% vs normal
training day.
8. CARRY 2 BOTTLES: One plain water + one electrolyte drink. Switch between them throughout practice. Never rely on one
source.

SECTION 12
Hostel-Friendly Cricket Nutrition
Practical, affordable nutrition for academy, college and boarding-school cricketers.
The majority of India's promising young cricketers live in hostel accommodation — whether at cricket academies, colleges
or coaching centres. Limited cooking facilities, mess food that is often high in oil and low in protein, peer pressure around
food choices, and tight budgets all create nutritional challenges. This section provides practical, affordable solutions for the
hostel cricketer.

SECTION 13
Low Budget Cricket Nutrition
200+ affordable foods for student athletes — all available at any Indian kirana or sabzi mandi.
Great nutrition doesn't require expensive supplements or exotic imports. India has one of the richest, most affordable
nutrition ecosystems in the world. The 200+ foods below are available at any Indian kirana store, sabzi mandi or college
canteen for under n30–50 per serving. Many Olympic and Test level Indian cricketers built their physical base entirely on
these foods before any supplements entered their diet. Master these and you will have everything you need.

SECTION 14
Match Day Nutrition
Before match · During match · After match — complete protocol for every format.
Match day nutrition can be the difference between a 50 and a century, a maiden over and a boundary. The science is clear:
what you eat in the 24 hours before, during and after a match significantly determines energy availability, concentration
span, recovery speed and injury risk. This section provides India-specific protocols for different match formats and
scenarios.

n GOLDEN RULE: Never try a new food, supplement or routine on match day. Only eat foods that you have eaten during
practice and know your body tolerates well. Match day is the day to execute — not to experiment.

SECTION 15
Cricket Nutrition Mistakes
Top 100 mistakes young Indian cricketers make — and how to correct them.
The fastest way to elevate your cricket nutrition is not to add something new — it is to stop doing the things that actively
harm your performance. These 100 mistakes were compiled from conversations with Indian coaches, sports dietitians and
cricketers at every level from Under-16 district cricket to IPL. Most mistakes are habitual and easy to fix once recognized.
n HYDRATION MISTAKES
1. Relying on thirst as the only signal to drink — thirst appears AFTER 1.5% dehydration, which already impairs performance.
2. Drinking only plain water during 3+ hour matches — causes sodium dilution (hyponatremia); always add electrolytes.
3. Not drinking any fluid in the first hour of practice because 'not feeling thirsty' — by then, you are already dehydrated.
4. Drinking cold soda (cola/Limca/Sprite) during match breaks — high fructose + carbonation causes GI bloating; avoid
completely.
5. Skipping the morning 500ml of water before match — arriving at the ground dehydrated before play starts.
6. Consuming alcohol the night before a match — diuretic that suppresses vasopressin; severely worsens next-day hydration.
7. Not checking urine colour before training — dark yellow urine = already dehydrated; train pale yellow always.
8. Drinking 2 litres of water in 30 minutes after heat exposure — hyponatremia risk; rehydrate gradually over 3-4 hours.
9. Not bringing a personal water bottle to every practice — relying on team supply leads to under-drinking consistently.
10. Confusing tea/coffee hydration with water hydration — moderate caffeine is net neutral, but 4+ cups are diuretic.
n PRE-MATCH NUTRITION MISTAKES
11. Eating a heavy biryani or full restaurant meal 1-2 hours before a match — insufficient digestion time; blood diverted from
muscles to gut.
12. Skipping breakfast on match day to feel 'light' — glycogen depleted by morning; severe concentration drop by 2nd session.
13. Trying a completely new food on match morning — GI risk; only eat foods tested during practice sessions.
14. Eating very spicy curry the night before a match — disrupts sleep + GI motility; avoid on match eve.
15. Not eating for 4+ hours before a long day's match — glycogen falls below threshold; batting and bowling both impaired.
16. Eating too much raw high-fibre food (cabbage, broccoli) 2h pre-match — causes bloating and gas during batting.
17. Relying on energy drinks (Red Bull / Monster) for pre-match energy — tachycardia + crash risk; unsafe dose of caffeine +
sugar combo.
18. Eating a protein shake INSTEAD of a carb meal pre-match — protein alone does not fuel batting or bowling intensity.
19. Eating a cold, poorly stored hostel tiffin from morning — GI infection risk in Indian climate; never eat food left over 4+ hours
unrefrigerated.
20. Drinking 3-4 cups of chai before match for energy — excessive caffeine anxiety + subsequent crash during key moments.
n PROTEIN MYTHS & MISTAKES
21. Believing eating more protein automatically builds more muscle — without adequate sleep + resistance training + calories,
protein alone does nothing.
22. Taking 3-4 protein scoops daily without training resistance — unused amino acids are metabolised and stored as fat or
excreted.
23. Drinking a protein shake and then eating nothing else for 2 hours — protein needs carbs to create an insulin spike for
muscle delivery.
Page 75
ZITLAS — Cricket Nutrition Knowledge Book  |  Educational Reference  |  Confidential
24. Avoiding eggs because of cholesterol fear — dietary cholesterol does not raise blood cholesterol in healthy athletes; eggs
are essential.
25. Believing plant protein is inferior — complete plant protein (soy, quinoa, amaranth) fully supports muscle synthesis when
adequate leucine is present.
26. Skipping post-training protein because 'I'll eat at dinner in 3 hours' — 30-minute muscle protein synthesis window is the
highest of the day.
27. Using mass gainer as a meal substitute — most gainers are predominantly sugar; use only as a calorie top-up, not a food
replacement.
28. Believing more protein = faster recovery — rate is limited by leucine dose (2.5g per meal triggers MPS); beyond that, excess
is wasteful.
29. Not eating any protein at breakfast — breakfast protein is the most under-consumed meal; sets anabolic tone for the day.
30. Thinking vegetarian diet cannot support high-level cricket — soy chunks + quinoa + dal + paneer + eggs = fully adequate;
many Test cricketers are vegetarian.
n CARBOHYDRATE MISTAKES
31. Going low-carb or keto during the cricket season — carbohydrates are the primary fuel for all cricket intensity; low-carb
causes pace, shot power and sprint speed to drop.
32. Eating only white rice with no protein or fat — spikes blood sugar rapidly and creates a crash 90 minutes later; always pair
with protein.
33. Avoiding rice because it is 'fattening' — no single food is fattening; total calories in context matter; rice is the best glycogen
fuel for Indian cricketers.
34. Not eating carbs after 7pm — evening training is common; glycogen from dinner fuels the next morning's training.
35. Eating junk carbs (biscuits, chips, maida) as main energy source — micronutrient-poor; no vitamins or minerals; spike +
crash.
36. Skipping carbs before an early morning training session — even 1 banana = 27g carbs; empty-stomach training causes
muscle catabolism.
37. Replacing meals with glucose biscuits — marketing masquerades as health; high refined sugar, no meaningful micronutrient
base.
38. Not adjusting carb intake on rest days — on rest days, reduce carb intake by 20-30%; unused glycogen converts to fat.
39. Confusing 'high carb' with 'low quality' — complex carbs (dal, rajma, whole wheat) are high-quality; they are not the enemy.
40. Drinking fruit juice thinking it replaces fruit — juice has no fibre, higher glycaemic impact; eat the whole fruit for sustained
energy.
n FAT & MICRONUTRIENT MISTAKES
41. Avoiding all fats thinking it helps stay lean — fats are required for testosterone synthesis, Vit D absorption and joint health.
42. Using cheap refined vegetable oils only — trans fats in partial hydrogenation; cold-pressed mustard/groundnut oil is India's
best affordable option.
43. Not eating any fish or omega-3 source — omega-3 deficiency leads to chronic joint inflammation, slower recovery and poor
brain function.
44. Being afraid of ghee — 1 teaspoon per day provides butyrate, Vit K2 and CLA; traditional India uses it correctly in small
amounts.
45. Ignoring iron intake — iron deficiency is India's most common nutritional gap; anaemia reduces pace, endurance and
concentration significantly.
46. Not getting enough Vitamin D — most Indian cricketers, despite playing outdoors, have low Vit D due to high melanin +
inadequate dietary intake; supplement 2,000IU daily.
47. Not taking Vitamin D3 + K2 together — Vit D increases calcium absorption but K2 directs it to bones (not arteries); always
combine.
48. Eating the same narrow set of vegetables every day — micronutrient gaps develop; 5+ colours of vegetables weekly
ensures full spectrum.
Page 76
ZITLAS — Cricket Nutrition Knowledge Book  |  Educational Reference  |  Confidential
49. Skipping calcium intake because 'already drinking milk' — milk provides calcium but absorption requires Vit D and K2 and
adequate magnesium.
50. Not consuming zinc-rich foods regularly — zinc deficiency reduces testosterone, immunity and wound healing; pumpkin
seeds + meat correct this.
n SUPPLEMENT MISTAKES
51. Starting supplements before establishing a good base diet — supplements cannot fix a poor diet; food first, always.
52. Buying the cheapest whey protein without checking for third-party testing — contamination and underdosing are real; choose
tested brands.
53. Taking multiple fat burners, testosterone boosters and pre-workouts simultaneously — interactions and cardiovascular risks
are real; consult a doctor.
54. Believing creatine is a steroid or harmful — creatine monohydrate is the most studied and safest legal supplement; 5g daily
is standard and safe.
55. Cycling creatine on/off — creatine works via saturation; no cycling required; take 5g daily consistently.
56. Taking whey protein with hot liquid — heat denatures whey; always mix in cold or lukewarm water or milk.
57. Using supplements to compensate for poor sleep — no supplement overcomes sleep deprivation; sleep IS the performance
supplement.
58. Buying flavoured protein powder with 5+ types of sugar — choose isolate or concentrate with <5g sugar per scoop.
59. Not taking Vitamin D despite indoor lifestyle or dark skin tone — melanin blocks Vit D synthesis; supplement 2,000IU
year-round in India.
60. Spending budget on 10 supplements while neglecting quality food — n3,000/month on supplements + poor food = worse
than n3,000/month on quality dal+eggs+fish.
n RECOVERY MISTAKES
61. Not eating anything within 30 minutes post-training — the 30-min window has 2x higher muscle protein synthesis rate; miss
it = 50% slower recovery.
62. Drinking alcohol after a match as celebration — even 1-2 drinks reduce MPS by 30%, impair sleep architecture and double
inflammation markers.
63. Going straight to sleep after a hard day without eating — catabolism occurs overnight without pre-sleep protein; muscles
break down.
64. Eating an unhealthy takeaway as a 'post-match treat' — high trans fat + low nutrient junk impairs the recovery it should
support.
65. Sleeping less than 7 hours and expecting nutrition to compensate — sleep is the primary anabolic window; no nutrition can
replace it.
66. Not eating enough on rest days — muscles repair on rest days; calorie restriction on rest days slows muscle repair.
67. Icing injuries immediately without checking appropriateness — ice slows blood flow = reduces amino acid delivery = slows
tendon healing in first 72h (now debated).
68. Skipping the post-match meal because 'too tired' — fatigue is the indicator you NEED the nutrition; liquid options (shake,
milk) are valid.
69. Not using omega-3 consistently — omega-3 anti-inflammatory effect requires 4+ weeks of consistent use; irregular use
provides no benefit.
70. Ignoring gut health — gut microbiome is directly linked to inflammation, immunity and mental health; probiotics from curd +
fermented foods daily.
n MENTAL & LIFESTYLE NUTRITION MISTAKES
71. Associating certain foods with weakness or femininity (milk, curd, dal) — these are the foundation of Test match-level cricket
physical base.
72. Eating differently on match days vs practice days — your body cannot suddenly adapt; match-day performance reflects
weeks of daily nutrition.
Page 77
ZITLAS — Cricket Nutrition Knowledge Book  |  Educational Reference  |  Confidential
73. Skipping meals due to financial stress without seeking alternatives — sattu (n4/serving), eggs (n7/serving), boiled chana
(n5/serving) are complete protein at very low cost.
74. Following social media fitness influencer diets without personalisation — generic diets ignore your body type, training load
and Indian food context.
75. Eating heavily and then not training adequately — excess calories without training stimulus store as fat, not muscle.
76. Competing with teammates in supplement consumption rather than food quality — performance depends on habit
consistency, not one-upmanship.
77. Not eating because 'nervous' before a big match — pre-match nerves are normal; food is essential; choose light familiar
foods (banana, curd rice, oats).
78. Thinking body weight scale = fitness — a 2kg weight gain during heavy training phase is muscle + glycogen + water; it is
positive progress.
79. Ignoring a professional dietitian's advice and following parent/coach nutrition myths — 'eat less rice to run faster' is false; this
section is for correcting exactly such myths.
80. Believing that what works for your hero cricketer works for you — individual variation in metabolism, gut microbiome and
food tolerance is enormous; personalise.
n TIMING & STRUCTURE MISTAKES
81. Not eating within the 30-minute post-training window — the rate of glycogen resynthesis is 2x higher in this window than at
any other time.
82. Eating a massive dinner to 'make up' for skipped meals — the body can only use ~25-30g protein per meal for MPS; the rest
is excreted or converted.
83. Training on a completely empty stomach thinking it burns more fat — fat oxidation is marginally higher but muscle
catabolism and performance crash are significant trade-offs.
84. Not eating carbs before early morning practice (before 8am) — liver glycogen depleted overnight; even 1 banana prevents
performance drop.
85. Eating a large meal immediately before strength training — blood diverted to digestion impairs muscle contraction force; eat
2-3h before.
86. Taking protein pre-sleep but skipping the carb component — insulin from carbs helps direct amino acids into muscle; casein
alone is less effective.
87. Spacing meals more than 5 hours apart — muscle protein falls into negative balance; eat protein every 3-5 hours for
sustained anabolism.
88. Eating dinner at 11pm after a late match with no appetite — even a glass of warm milk + banana = better than nothing; small
recovery dose matters.
89. Not meal-prepping for busy tournament weeks — hunger + convenience = junk food; batch-cook 2 days' food before
tournament starts.
90. Ignoring nutrition on travel days — travel days are recovery days; miss nutrition on them and you arrive at the next venue
already depleted.
n WEIGHT MANAGEMENT MISTAKES
91. Crash dieting before trials or selection — muscle loss is faster than fat loss on crash diet; you appear smaller, not leaner,
and perform worse.
92. Weighing yourself immediately after a session and panicking — sweat weight loss is fluid, not fat; normal 1-2kg fluctuation
daily.
93. Refusing to eat 'heavy' foods like rice and roti to lose weight — carbohydrate restriction before training causes performance
crashes; timing matters more than avoiding.
94. Comparing your body type to international cricketers from different ethnic backgrounds — body composition benchmarks
vary significantly by ethnicity and position.
95. Cutting calories too aggressively and then bingeing on weekends — yo-yo eating destroys metabolic consistency and
creates guilt cycles that harm mental performance.
Page 78
ZITLAS — Cricket Nutrition Knowledge Book  |  Educational Reference  |  Confidential
96. Thinking sweating in a sauna or with extra clothing burns meaningful fat — water weight only; rehydrated within hours; zero
fat lost.
97. Being afraid to gain weight during heavy training blocks — 2-4kg gain in a 6-week strength phase is expected and positive
(muscle + glycogen + water).
98. Following a teammate's diet without considering different positions, roles and body types — a fast bowler needs different
nutrition to an opening batsman.
99. Using diuretics or laxatives to 'make weight' — dangerous; electrolyte imbalance causes cardiac arrhythmia and severe
performance crash.
100. Ignoring hunger cues completely — persistent hunger = body sending urgent signal; chronic under-eating shrinks muscle
and drops testosterone.
Page 79
ZITLAS — Cricket Nutrition Knowledge Book  |  Educational Reference  |  Confidential
